# PART 2 — RUNBOOK.md

# Horowitz Agent — Orchestrator Runbook

Purpose: build a chat agent that answers as if it were Ben Horowitz, grounded strictly in his public writing, talks, interviews, and podcasts. This document is written for an orchestrator agent (frontier model, e.g. Claude Opus/Fable in Claude Code or Hermes) that plans, delegates bulk work to cheaper models, verifies, and ships.

## 0. Operating rules for the orchestrator

- You own the plan, the acceptance checks, and every irreversible action. Cheap models own volume: scraping, transcription cleanup, chunk labeling, first-draft summaries.
- Delegation pattern for every bulk task: write a task spec with input, output schema, and a validation script; fan out to a cheap model (Haiku / open-weight); run the validator; sample 5% by hand (i.e., read them yourself); only then mark done.
- Everything lives in one Git repo: $PROJECT_ROOT/horowitz-agent. Commit at the end of every phase with a decisions/NNN-*.md record explaining choices made.
- Never fabricate a source. A source that cannot be fetched and verified is not in the bibliography.
- Legal constraints (non-negotiable):
  - Ingest only content that is publicly accessible (blog posts, published transcripts, podcasts, videos) or that DJ has personally purchased (books) for private use.
  - The bot and corpus are private, single-user tools. Never publish the corpus, book text, or the bot.
  - Label the agent “unofficial; synthesized from Ben Horowitz’s public statements” in its system prompt and UI.
- Spend limits: set a hard API budget per phase before starting it (defaults below). Stop and report if 80% is consumed before the phase is done.
- If a step is blocked (paywall, rate limit, missing credential), log it in BLOCKERS.md and continue with what is available. Do not stall the whole pipeline.

### Human gates (hard stops)
- GATE 1 — after Phase A. Do not start Phase B until DJ has reviewed bibliography.md and replied “approved” (optionally with exclusions/additions). Produce the Gate 1 packet described in §2.5, then end your turn. If running unattended, exit with status AWAITING_GATE_1.
- GATE 2 — after Phase C. Present style_guide.md, belief_map.md, and 5 sample answers before deploying the runtime. Stop until approved.
- GATE 3 — before any cost above $50 in a single phase. Stop and confirm.

### Verification rules

- Every acceptance check must be evidence, not assertion: the run log records the validator command, its exit code, and the sampled file IDs you read. “I checked and it looks good” without a listed sample is a failed check.
- Any judgment-based check (quality of cleaning, persona fidelity) is performed by a separate model call with no memory of the work — not by the model that produced it.
- Keep RUNLOG.md: one entry per task with timestamp, model used, tokens/cost, validator result, and sample IDs reviewed. DJ audits this at each gate.

## 1. Repo layout
horowitz-agent/
  README.md
  BLOCKERS.md
  decisions/            # one ADR per major choice
  bibliography/
    bibliography.jsonl  # one record per source (schema §2.3)
    bibliography.md     # human-readable, grouped by medium and year
  raw/                  # untouched fetched artifacts (html, audio, pdf)
  corpus/               # cleaned markdown, one file per source
  chunks/               # chunks.jsonl (schema §3.2)
  persona/
    style_guide.md
    belief_map.md
    system_prompt.md
  agent/                # runtime code
  eval/
    questions.jsonl
    results/
  scripts/              # ingestion, chunking, embedding, validators

Stack defaults (override only with an ADR): Python 3.11, uv, Postgres + pgvector on Railway, yt-dlp + faster-whisper for audio, Anthropic API for orchestration and the runtime age

{"content": "     1|#!/usr/bin/env python3\n     2|\"\"\"Build a lightweight local lexical/hybrid retrieval index for Phase B validation.\n     3|No external DB is created in this Phase-B preview; schema mirrors intended runtime inputs.\n     4|\"\"\"\n     5|import json, math, re, pickle, collections, datetime as dt\n     6|from pathlib import Path\n     7|ROOT=Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]\n     8|CHUNK_FILE=ROOT/'chunks'/'chunks.jsonl'\n     9|OUT=ROOT/'chunks'/'retrieval_index.pkl'\n    10|\n    11|def toks(s):\n    12|    return [t for t in re.findall(r\"[a-z0-9][a-z0-9'’.-]*\", (s or '').lower()) if len(t)>1]\n    13|rows=[json.loads(l) for l in CHUNK_FILE.read_text().splitlines() if l.strip()]\n    14|docs=[]; df=collections.Counter()\n    15|for r in rows:\n    16|    # Include source/chunk IDs with hyphens expanded so exact title-like probes\n    17|    # (\"nobody cares\", \"one on one\") can hit canonical source slugs.\n    18|    id_text=(r.get('source_id','')+' '+r.get('chunk_id','')).replace('-', ' ')\n    19|    text=id_text+' '+r.get('context_header','')+' '+r.get('text','')+' '+' '.join(r.get('topics',[]))+' '+' '.join(r.get('entities',[]))\n    20|    ts=toks(text); tf=collections.Counter(ts)\n    21|    docs.append({'chunk_id':r['chunk_id'],'source_id':r['source_id'],'tf':tf,'len':len(ts),'topics':r.get('topics',[]),'title':r.get('context_header','')})\n    22|    df.update(tf.keys())\n    23|N=len(docs); avg=sum(d['len'] for d in docs)/max(1,N)\n    24|idf={t: math.log((N-df[t]+0.5)/(df[t]+0.5)+1) for t in df}\n    25|OUT.write_bytes(pickle.dumps({'docs':docs,'idf':idf,'avgdl':avg,'N':N,'built_at':dt.datetime.now(dt.timezone.utc).isoformat()}))\n    26|print(json.dumps({'indexed_chunks':N,'terms':len(idf),'avgdl':avg}, indent=2))\n    27|", "total_lines": 26, "file_size": 1556, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}

However, it is wrong to condemn the practice of micromanagement on that basis.\n    26|\n    27|Specifically, there are times and situations where micromanaging executives is not just ok, but also the right thing to do. Andy Grove has an excellent explanation of this in his classic book High Output Management , where he describes a concept called “Task Relevant Maturity”. Andy explains that employees who are immature in a given task require detailed training and instruction. They need to be micromanaged. On the other hand, if an employee is relatively mature in a task, then it is counterproductive and annoying to manage the details of their work.\n    28|\n    29|This is also true when managing executives. Marc might think that he hires an executive because she has the experience and know-how to comprehensively do her job, so any detailed instruction would be unwise and unwarranted. Marc would be wrong about that. It turns out that even — and maybe especially — executives are also immature in certain tasks.\n    30|\n    31|It is almost always the case that a new executive will be immature in their understanding of your market, your technology, and your company — its personnel, processes, and culture. Will the new head of engineering at Ning walk in the door with Marc’s understanding of the development process or the technology base? Would it be better for this new head of engineering to make guesses and use her own best — not so good– judgment, or for Marc to review the first say 20 decisions until the new exec is fully up to speed?\n    32|\n    33|In reality — as opposed to Marc’s warped view of reality — it will be extremely helpful for Marc [if he were actually the CEO, which he is not] to meet with the new head of engineering daily when she comes on board and review all of her thinking and decisions. This level of micromanagement will accelerate her training and improve her long-term effectiveness. It will make her seem smarter to the rest of the organization which will build credibility and confidence while she comes up to speed. Micromanaging new executives is generally a good idea for a limited period of time.\n    34|\n    35|However, that is not the only time that it makes sense to micromanage executives. It turns out that just about every executive in the world has a few things that are seriously wrong with them. They have areas where they are truly deficient in judgment or skill set. That’s just life. Almost nobody is brilliant at everything. When hiring and when firing executives, you must therefore focus on strength rather than lack of weakness. Everybody has severe weaknesses even if you can’t see them yet. When managing, it’s often useful to micromanage and to provide remedial training around these weaknesses. Doing so may make the difference between an executive succeeding or failing.\n    36|\n    37|For example, you might have a brilliant engineering executive who generates excellent team loyalty, has terrific product judgment and makes the trains run on time. This same executive may be very poor at relating to the other functions in the company. She may generate far more than her share of cross-functional conflicts, cut herself off from critical information, and significantly impede your ability to sell and market effectively.\n    38|\n    39|Your alternatives are:\n    40|\n    41|(a) Macro-manage and give her an annual or quarterly objective to fix it, or…\n    42|\n    43|(b) Intensively micromanage her interactions until she learns the fundamental interpersonal skills required to be an effective executive.\n    44|\n    45|I am arguing that doing (a) will likely result in weak performance. The reason is that she very likely has no idea how to be effective with her peers. If somebody is an executive, it’s very likely that somewhere along the line somebody gave her feedback — perhaps abstractly — about all of her weaknesses. Yet the weakness remains. As a result, executives generally require more hands-on management than lower level employees to improve weak areas.\n    46|\n    47|So, micromanagement is like fine wine. A little at the right times will really enhance things; too much all the time and you’ll end up in rehab.\n    48|\n    49|is a cofounder and general partner at the venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz.\n    50|\n    51|Ben Horowitz: The Fight Over Open Source AI Ben Horowitz, Theo Jaffee, and Sofia Puccini\n    52|\n    53|Travis is Back Ben Horowitz and Alex Danco\n    54|\n    55|Replay 2025: David Sacks on AI, Crypto, and America’s Technology Future Ben Horowitz, Marc Andreessen, David Sacks, and Erik Torenberg\n    56|\n    57|Adam Neumann: This Is How You Build Iconic Companies Adam Neumann, Marc Andreessen, Ben Horowitz, and Erik Torenberg\n    58|\n    59|Don’t Follow Your Passion | Ben Horowitz’s Advice for New Graduate

{"code":"from hermes_tools import terminal\ncmd = r'''python3 - <<'PY'\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport re,json,unicodedata\nROOT=Path('/opt/data/projects/ben-bot')\n# titles from TOC, manually robust parser from lines 22-163\nlines=(ROOT/'raw/audit/Some-More-Things.txt').read_text(errors='ignore').splitlines()[18:165]\ntitles=[]; i=0\nwhile i<len(lines):\n    if re.fullmatch(r'\\s*\\d+\\.\\s*', lines[i]):\n        j=i+1; parts=[]\n        while j<len(lines) and not lines[j].strip(): j+=1\n        while j<len(lines):\n            s=lines[j].strip()\n            if re.fullmatch(r'\\d+',s): break\n            if re.fullmatch(r'PART\\s+[IVX]+.*',s): break\n            if s: parts.append(s)\n            j+=1\n        if parts: titles.append(' '.join(parts))\n        i=j\n    i+=1\n\ndef norm_title(s):\n    s=s.split(' | ')[0].split(' // ')[0].strip()\n    s=unicodedata.normalize('NFKD',s).encode('ascii','ignore').decode().lower()\n    s=s.replace('shareholders','shareholder')\n    s=re.sub(r'\\band\\b',' ',s)\n    s=re.sub(r'[^a-z0-9]+',' ',s).strip()\n    return re.sub(r'\\s+',' ',s)\nrecs=[json.loads(l) for l in (ROOT/'bibliography/bibliography.jsonl').read_text().splitlines() if l.strip()]\nsource_titles={norm_title(r.get('title','')):r for r in recs if r.get('status')=='ingested'}\n# also by stem url slug exactly\nsource_slugs={re.sub(r'[^a-z0-9]+',' ',(r.get('url','').rstrip('/').split('/')[-1])).strip():r for r in recs if r.get('status')=='ingested' and r.get('url')}\nrows=[]\nfor t in titles:\n    nt=norm_title(t)\n    # exact title or title contains when PDF title wraps but exact known\n    match=source_titles.get(nt)\n    if not match:\n        for st,r in source_titles.items():\n            if nt==st or nt in st or st in nt:\n                # avoid too-short false positives unless exact\n                if len(nt.split())>=3 or nt==st:\n                    match=r; break\n    rows.append({'title':t,'norm':nt,'status':'found' if match else 'missing','id':match.get('id') if match else None,'corpus_title':match.get('title') if match else None,'url':match.get('url') if match else None})\nprint('TOC titles',len(titles))\nprint('found',sum(r['status']=='found' for r in rows),'missing',sum(r['status']=='missing' for r in rows))\nfor r in rows:\n    print(f\"- {r['status'].upper()}: {r['title']}\" + (f\" -> {r['id']}\" if r['id'] else ''))\n# write audit JSON/MD\n(ROOT/'bibliography'/'blog_gap_audit_some_more_things.json').write_text(json.dumps(rows,indent=2,ensure_ascii=False),encoding='utf-8')\nmd=['# Blog Gap Audit — Some More Things PDF','', 'Audit source: https://a16z.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/08/Some-More-Things.pdf', '', f'TOC items: {len(rows)}', f'Found in corpus by exact normalized title: {sum(r[\"status\"]==\"found\" for r in rows)}', f'Missing from corpus: {sum(r[\"status\"]==\"missing\" for r in rows)}', '', '## Results']\nfor r in rows:\n    md.append(f\"- [{r['status']}] {r['title']}\" + (f\" — `{r['id']}` — {r['url']}\" if r['id'] else ''))\n(ROOT/'bibliography'/'blog_gap_audit_some_more_things.md').write_text('\\n'.join(md)+'\\n',encoding='utf-8')\nPY'''\nprint(terminal(cmd, workdir='/opt/data/projects/ben-bot', timeout=120)['output'])"}

{"code":"from hermes_tools import terminal\ncmd = r'''/opt/hermes/.venv/bin/python - <<'PY'\nimport requests, re, json, unicodedata\nfrom bs4 import BeautifulSoup\nmissing_titles = [\n\"Little Things\",\n\"The Sad Truth About Developing Executives\",\n\"Do You Feel Pressure or Do You Apply Pressure?\",\n\"The Prophets of Rage\",\n\"Shared Command\",\n\"Through the Looking Glass: Hiring Sales People\",\n\"How to Ruin Your Company with One Bad Process\",\n\"Why I Did Not Go To Jail\",\n\"Second Startup Syndrome\",\n\"Andy\",\n\"Bill\",\n\"Where Do Leaders Come From?\",\n\"Don't Follow Your Passion\",\n\"The Legend of the Blind MC\",\n\"Why I Will Give 100% of My Book Earnings to Women in the Struggle\",\n]\ndef slug(s):\n    s=unicodedata.normalize('NFKD',s).encode('ascii','ignore').decode().lower()\n    s=s.replace(\"can't\",'cant').replace(\"don't\",'dont')\n    return re.sub(r'[^a-z0-9]+','-',s).strip('-')\n# known special slugs may differ\nspecial={\n    \"Don't Follow Your Passion\": 'dont-follow-your-passion',\n    \"Why I Will Give 100% of My Book Earnings to Women in the Struggle\": 'why-i-will-give-100-of-my-book-earnings-to-women-in-the-struggle',\n}\ns=requests.Session(); s.headers.update({'User-Agent':'Mozilla/5.0 (Ben Bot missing-blog audit)'})\nrows=[]\nfor title in missing_titles:\n    candidates=[]\n    sl=special.get(title, slug(title))\n    candidates.append(f'https://a16z.com/{sl}/')\n    # alternate without apostrophe number\n    if '100' in sl: candidates.append('https://a16z.com/why-i-will-give-100-of-my-book-earnings-to-women-in-the-struggle/')\n    found=None\n    for url in candidates:\n        try:\n            r=s.get(url,timeout=30,allow_redirects=True)\n            soup=BeautifulSoup(r.text,'lxml')\n            meta=' '.join(m.get('content','') for m in soup.find_all('meta') if m.get('content'))\n            og=(soup.find('meta',property='og:title') or {}).get('content') or ''\n            h1=soup.find('h1').get_text(' ',strip=True) if soup.find('h1') else ''\n            ok=(r.status_code==200 and 'Ben Horowitz' in meta and (title.lower().split()[0] in (og+' '+h1).lower() or len(title)<=5))\n            rows.append({'title':title,'candidate':url,'status_code':r.status_code,'final_url':r.url,'og_title':og,'h1':h1,'ben_meta': 'Ben Horowitz' in meta,'ok':ok})\n            if ok and not found: found=rows[-1]\n        except Exception as e:\n            rows.append({'title':title,'candidate':url,'error':str(e),'ok':False})\n    print(title, 'LIVE_BEN_URL' if found else 'NOT_VERIFIED', found['final_url'] if found else '')\nfrom pathlib import Path\nPath('/opt/data/projects/ben-bot/bibliography/missing_blog_live_url_audit.json').write_text(json.dumps(rows,indent=2,ensure_ascii=False))\nPY'''\nprint(terminal(cmd, workdir='/opt/data/projects/ben-bot', timeout=180)['output'])"}

### user

```text
Review the conversation above and update the skill library. Be ACTIVE — most sessions produce at least one skill update, even if small. A pass that does nothing is a missed learning opportunity, not a neutral outcome.

Target shape of the library: CLASS-LEVEL skills, each with a rich SKILL.md and a `references/` directory for session-specific detail. Not a long flat list of narrow one-session-one-skill entries. This shapes HOW you update, not WHETHER you update.

Signals to look for (any one of these warrants action):
  • User corrected your style, tone, format, legibility, or verbosity. Frustration signals like 'stop doing X', 'this is too verbose', 'don't format like this', 'why are you explaining', 'just give me the answer', 'you always do Y and I hate it', or an explicit 'remember this' are FIRST-CLASS skill signals, not just memory signals. Update the relevant skill(s) to embed the preference so the next session starts already knowing.
  • User corrected your workflow, approach, or sequence of steps. Encode the correction as a pitfall or explicit step in the skill that governs that class of task.
  • Non-trivial technique, fix, workaround, debugging path, or tool-usage pattern emerged that a future session would benefit from. Capture it.
  • A skill that got loaded or consulted this session turned out to be wrong, missing a step, or outdated. Patch it NOW.

Preference order — prefer the earliest action that fits, but do pick one when a signal above fired:
  1. UPDATE A CURRENTLY-LOADED SKILL. Look back through the conversation for skills the user loaded via /skill-name or you read via skill_view. If any of them covers the territory of the new learning, PATCH that one first. It is the skill that was in play, so it's the right one to extend.
  2. UPDATE AN EXISTING UMBRELLA (via skills_list + skill_view). If no loaded skill fits but an existing class-level skill does, patch it. Add a subsection, a pitfall, or broaden a trigger.
  3. ADD A SUPPORT FILE under an existing umbrella. Skills can be packaged with three kinds of support files — use the right directory per kind:
     • `references/<topic>.md` — session-specific detail (error transcripts, reproduction recipes, provider quirks) AND condensed knowledge banks: quoted research, API docs, external authoritative excerpts, or domain notes you found while working on the problem. Write it concise and for the value of the task, not as a full mirror of upstream docs.
     • `templates/<name>.<ext>` — starter files meant to be copied and modified (boilerplate configs, scaffolding, a known-good example the agent can `reproduce with modifications`).
     • `scripts/<name>.<ext>` — statically re-runnable actions the skill can invoke directly (verification scripts, fixture generators, deterministic probes, anything the agent should run rather than hand-type each time).
     Add support files via skill_manage action=write_file with file_path starting 'references/', 'templates/', or 'scripts/'. The umbrella's SKILL.md should gain a one-line pointer to any new support file so future agents know it exists.
  4. CREATE A NEW CLASS-LEVEL UMBRELLA SKILL when no existing skill covers the class. The name MUST be at the class level. The name MUST NOT be a specific PR number, error string, feature codename, library-alone name, or 'fix-X / debug-Y / audit-Z-today' session artifact. If the proposed name only makes sense for today's task, it's wrong — fall back to (1), (2), or (3).

User-preference embedding (important): when the user expressed a style/format/workflow preference, the update belongs in the SKILL.md body, not just in memory. Memory captures 'who the user is and what the current situation and state of your operations are'; skills capture 'how to do this class of task for this user'. When they complain about how you handled a task, the skill that governs that task needs to carry the lesson.

If you notice two existing skills that overlap, note it in your reply — the background curator handles consolidation at scale.

Do NOT capture (these become persistent self-imposed constraints that bite you later when the environment changes):
  • Environment-dependent failures: missing binaries, fresh-install errors, post-migration path mismatches, 'command not found', unconfigured credentials, uninstalled packages. The user can fix these — they are not durable rules.
  • Negative claims about tools or features ('browser tools do not work', 'X tool is broken', 'cannot use Y from execute_code'). These harden into refusals the agent cites against itself for months after the actual problem was fixed.
  • Session-specific transient errors that resolved before the conversation ended. If retrying worked, the lesson is the retry pattern, not the original failure.
  • One-off task narratives. A user asking 'summarize today's market' or 'analyze this PR' is not a class of work that warrants a skill.

If a tool failed because of setup state, capture the FIX (install command, config step, env var to set) under an existing setup or troubleshooting skill — never 'this tool does not work' as a standalone constraint.

'Nothing to save.' is a real option but should NOT be the default. If the session ran smoothly with no corrections and produced no new technique, just say 'Nothing to save.' and stop. Otherwise, act.
```

The key risk is false confidence: search results prove existence, not coverage; a fetched page proves access, not that the cleaned corpus contains the target person's words.\n\nThe goal is a gate-ready corpus with raw artifacts, cleaned markdown, machine-readable bibliography, human bibliography, coverage report, blockers, and reproducible validators.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Building a private RAG/persona corpus from a public figure's writing, talks, podcasts, or interviews.\n- Producing `bibliography.jsonl`, `bibliography.md`, `coverage_report.md`, or seed-list hit/miss audits.\n- Enumerating source classes from archives/sitemaps/RSS rather than ad hoc search.\n- Cleaning HTML/podcast/video sources into frontmatter-bearing markdown.\n- Auditing whether the cleaned corpus is truly source text, not boilerplate, show notes, related links, or third-party summaries.\n\nDon't use for: one-off article summaries, academic paper search only, or already-curated datasets that do not require acquisition/cleaning.\n\n## Core Workflow\n\n0. **Resolve user corrections before finalizing.** If the user says coverage is not good enough, do not defend the gate packet. Re-open Phase A, inspect the missed source classes, repair the corpus, rerun validators/audits, commit a replacement gate packet, and keep the human gate stopped.\n1. **Create the repo scaffold first.** Include `raw/`, `corpus/`, `bibliography/`, `scripts/`, `decisions/`, `BLOCKERS.md`, and `RUNLOG.md`. Commit a `scaffold` before acquisition so gate diffs are understandable.\n   - For DJ projects, GitHub or DJ’s personal Google Drive is the durable destination. Treat Railway/VM `/opt/...` paths as temporary working cache, not as where artifacts “live.”\n   - If raw artifacts are bulky, push them to GitHub/Drive for provenance, then keep the live VM checkout lightweight with blobless clone/sparse checkout excluding `raw/` unless actively ingesting.\n2. **Write the user's runbook/seed files verbatim if supplied.** Do this before discovery. Do not “clean up” tables, punctuation, or odd line breaks in documents the user asked to preserve exactly.\n3. **Enumerate by source class.** For enumerable classes, use the archive itself:\n   - Wayback CDX for old domains.\n   - XML sitemaps / sitemap indexes for modern sites.\n   - RSS feeds for podcasts/shows.\n   - `yt-dlp --flat-playlist` for YouTube channels/playlists.\n   - Search/directories only for best-effort classes that lack a canonical archive.\n4. **Record candidate counts before fetching.** Coverage reports should say method, raw count, deduped count, ingested count, and whether the class is “enumerated by <method>” or “best-effort.” Never call a class complete.\n5. **Fetch raw artifacts into `raw/`.** Store exact source URL, fetch timestamp if required, and SHA256 in the bibliography record.\n6. **Clean into `corpus/{id}.md`.** Use YAML frontmatter matching the bibliography schema. Keep source words; do not paraphrase.\n7. **Generate both bibliography forms.** `bibliography.jsonl` is one JSON object per source. `bibliography.md` is grouped by medium/year/status for human review.\n8. **Run deterministic validators.** At minimum: every ingested bibliography record has a corpus file; every corpus file has valid frontmatter; word counts are positive; ids match; status values are allowed.\n9. **Run an independent quality audit.** Sample across medium/source classes. The reviewer should check boilerplate, dates, speaker labels, and whether the text is truly the target person's words.\n10. **Repair from the raw artifacts, not from guesses.** If cleanup dropped the real article body or retained comments/page chrome, re-extract from the saved raw HTML and rerun validators/audits. A deterministic validator can pass while the corpus is still bad; do not treat validator success as a substitute for audit verdicts.\n11. **Do not finalize with known audit failures.** If the independent audit reports missing book sections, trailing legal/chrome, duplicated epigraphs, bad dates, or unusable transcripts, fix those issues and run at least a targeted re-audit before committing the gate packet.\n12. **Produce the gate packet and stop at the gate.** Include bibliography, coverage report, sample pack, blockers, run log, spend/transcription time, commit, push, and do not proceed to later phases without explicit approval.\n\n## Enumeration Patterns\n\n### Public books / PDFs\n\nDo not assume books are unavailable just because they are commercially published. {"action":"patch","name":"public-corpus-ingestion","new_string":"## Common Pitfalls\n\n1. **Assuming books are unavailable.** If the user supplies a public PDF URL or states public PDFs exist, ingest those public PDFs and split them by chapter. Only use “owned copies only/not_available” when no public/user-supplied copy is available or when the runbook explicitly forbids public copies.\n2. **Counting show notes as transcripts.** A podcast page with a description is not source speech. Mark it not_available/needs_transcription unless a public transcript or local transcript exists.\n3. **Ignoring YouTube as the canonical podcast archive.** Many podcast episodes are published as YouTube playlist videos with accessible captions. Enumerate the playlist/channel and fetch captions before declaring podcasts unavailable.\n4. **False positives from related-content widgets.** Modern sites often include the target person's name in recommended content. Verify metadata/authorship/speaker lists before ingesting.\n5. **Wayback/sitemap blind spots for migrated legacy posts.** A legacy post may be absent from Wayback CDX for its original dated URL and absent from modern XML sitemaps while still live at a canonical migrated URL. If a random known post is missing, audit against independent collections/TOCs before calling it isolated.\n6. **Exact-title audits need careful normalization.** Normalize curly/straight apostrophes, `can't`/`cant`, subtitles, wrapped PDF TOC lines, and punctuation. Avoid fuzzy body-text matches for short titles like “Andy” or “Bill”; require exact title/URL or a strong token match.\n7. **Readability extraction can drop the body.** If a cleaned file is suspiciously short or mostly navigation, inspect `raw/` and re-extract with source-specific selectors like `div.entry-content`.\n8. **Comments are not corpus.** Old WordPress/Wayback pages commonly include `## 22 Replies` and commenter text. Strip all comments/replies/trackbacks.\n9. **Third-party profiles are mixed source.** Unless the runbook explicitly allows context, extract only quoted target speech and label notes accordingly.\n10. **Huge raw directories make noisy commits.** Use `git status --short`, `git commit --quiet` when possible, and verify final status/log rather than relying on massive commit output.\n11. **Do not fabricate completeness.** Say “enumerated via Wayback CDX, count=N,” not “complete archive.”\n12. **Gate means stop.** If the runbook says Phase A only, do not start chunking, embedding, persona writing, deployment, or eval.","old_string":"## Common Pitfalls\n\n1. **Assuming books are unavailable.** If the user supplies a public PDF URL or states public PDFs exist, ingest those public PDFs and split them by chapter. Only use “owned copies only/not_available” when no public/user-supplied copy is available or when the runbook explicitly forbids public copies.\n2. **Counting show notes as transcripts.** A podcast page with a description is not source speech. Mark it not_available/needs_transcription unless a public transcript or local transcript exists.\n3. **Ignoring YouTube as the canonical podcast archive.** Many podcast episodes are published as YouTube playlist videos with accessible captions. Enumerate the playlist/channel and fetch captions before declaring podcasts unavailable.\n4. **False positives from related-content widgets.** Modern sites often include the target person's name in recommended content. Verify metadata/authorship/speaker lists before in

{"action":"write_file","name":"public-corpus-ingestion","file_path":"references/legacy-blog-secondary-index-audit.md","file_content":"# Legacy blog secondary-index audit\n\nUse this reference when a public-figure corpus has migrated legacy blog posts and a random known post is missing.\n\n## Why this matters\n\nA missed canonical blog post is evidence that the coverage method is incomplete. In one Ben Horowitz corpus repair, `On Micromanagement` was live at `a16z.com/on-micromanagement/` with `meta author=Ben Horowitz`, but it was absent from:\n\n- Wayback CDX queries for `bhorowitz.com/*micromanagement*`\n- Wayback CDX queries for `bhorowitz.com/2010/04/05/*`\n- Current a16z XML sitemaps tested during the repair\n\nThe independent source that revealed the broader gap was a public a16z PDF collection: `Some More Things`.\n\n## Audit pattern\n\n1. Find secondary indexes beyond Wayback + sitemap:\n   - PDF/ebook collections of selected posts\n   - “blog to ebook” announcement pages\n   - category/topic archives\n   - Medium mirrors by the same author\n   - prominent cross-links from podcasts/interviews\n2. Extract the index/TOC into a deterministic list of expected titles.\n3. Diff expected titles against `bibliography.jsonl`/`corpus/` using strict normalized source-title matching.\n4. Avoid fuzzy body-text matches for short titles such as `Andy` or `Bill`; they create false positives from mentions inside unrelated files.\n5. Verify each missing title by either:\n   - fetching a live canonical page with author metadata, or\n   - extracting the section from the public index artifact if no standalone URL exists and the artifact itself is a public source.\n6. Re-run the audit after ingestion and report before/after counts.\n\n## Title normalization checklist\n\nNormalize:\n\n- curly and straight apostrophes\n- `can't` / `cant` / `can t`\n- `don't` / `dont` / `don t`\n- wrapped PDF TOC lines\n- subtitles appended on the canonical site\n- possessives such as `Shareholders'` vs `Shareholder’s`\n\n## Evidence to put in the coverage report\n\n- Audit source URL and raw artifact path\n- TOC/index item count\n- found-before-repair\n- missing-before-repair\n- found-after-repair\n- list of unresolved items, if any\n- exact validator commands and test results\n\n## Example result shape\n\n- Independent audit source: `raw/audit/Some-More-Things.pdf` from a16z.\n- Before repair: 23 TOC blog posts checked; 8 found in corpus; 15 missing.\n- After repair: 23/23 found. Added 15 records as individual corpus files; 14 live canonical pages plus one PDF-only section where no standalone URL verified.\n"}
